You also suffer a `caravan_robbery` crime (10 heat with its realm, 22 if wardens or a town saw it, which also reports the crime and can bring a bounty) and lose 15 reputation with the realm. The road is shunned and the destination goes without. A failed robbery still costs half the heat and reputation.

### Merchant Prince: Industry, Warehouses and Contracts

Beyond buying and selling, a rich trader can work a town's market from the inside. Open a town's **Trade** window and use its **🏭 Invest**, **📦 Warehouse** and **📜 Contracts** tabs (the **📒 Holdings** tab lists everything you own, anywhere). The Steward's Counsel warns of contracts falling due, rent owed, stock spoiling and works at risk. Tuned in `data/merchantPrince.json`.

**Industry.** Fund works in a town: a grain mill, bakehouse, brewhouse, charcoal kilns, distillery, weaving shed, tailors' hall, creamery, tannery, smithy, armoury, bronze foundry or jewellers' row. Each one is one of the town's own trades (the recipes above), and needs a big enough town. Each level costs gold plus building materials from your packs, adds a few runs a day, and costs 35% more than the last. A works can grow to 3 levels (the jewellers to 2).

- **How it runs.** On market day the works run after the town's own trades, on the town's real stock:
  - First they take over a share of the work the town's own trade did that turn: the town's craftsmen bring their work to the better-found works. The share is the works' runs against the works' and the trade's together, so a level takes nearly its full capacity from a big, busy trade and only a little from a small or idle one. The stores end as the trade left them; only the margin is yours.
  - Then, with capacity to spare, they run on top, taking their inputs only while the town keeps at least half its target of them.
  - They stop while their goods glut the stalls (twice the target).
  - They stop when a run no longer adds value at today's prices, after the wages of its hands.
  - They run only as far as the town's artisans can staff them, and stand idle while the artisans strike (see [Labour, Classes and Migration](#labour-classes-and-migration)).
- **What it pays.** You get **52%** of the value the works added (the output's worth less the inputs', at the town's prices), and out of it you pay the hands the works hire at the town's craft wage. The crown takes a **tithe** of your share: 10%, +15% while the realm is at war, and +20% while its treasury is in crisis. If you rule a realm whose court enforces *Merchant charters*, foreign crowns take only half.
- **Your own towns.** In a town you hold, works cost 15% less, pay you **72%** (less the wages), and owe no tithe. There is no tithe anywhere in a realm you rule.
- **Choosing a town.** The tab shows each trade's inputs and outputs with their stock labels, what a run adds at today's prices, and how much the town's own trade already works. A level in one of the great towns repays itself in about 35–55 days (a bakehouse about 40, a tannery about 55; a little faster once the towns have grown). In a small town, or one short of the inputs, it may barely turn: a brewhouse, mill, tannery or armoury in an ordinary town takes 100 days or more. Weaving sheds and smithies live on the wool and iron of the hinterland: a smithy in a town fed by an iron mine repays in about 15–55 days (the larger the town, the faster), in a small town with little use for tools 60–80. A bronze foundry needs a copper mine nearby. The jewellers' row cuts gems into luxuries: the craftsmen make fewer luxuries outright than they used to, so the stalls are no longer glutted with them and there are gems to cut late in the game; a fashion for jewelled finery makes both dearer for a season, and pious plainness cheaper.
- **Losing it.**
  - A realm at war with you, or with the realm you are sworn to, seizes your works at once.
  - A crown whose treasury is in crisis may seize them at its weekly reckoning.
  - If your reputation in the realm stays below −30 for 10 days, the town's guilds expropriate them. The Counsel warns you below −20.
  - Seized works keep running for their new owner.
  - As a journeyman or better of the town guild of the works' craft (see Town Guilds), a crisis seizure is half as likely (a third for a master, a fifth for its guildmaster), and expropriation waits twice as long. As an outsider, the guild may feud with works that undercut it.
- **Damage.**
  - A raid or sack that loots the town's stores also damages the works. So does a bandit raid that gets into one of your holdings.
  - A siege of the town idles them and wears them 3% a day.
  - Storm and quake damage to the town slows them.
  - Repairs cost half the works' price times the damage.
  - You can sell your stake back to the guild for 40% of what you put in, times its repair.

**Warehouses.** Rent a **store-room** (150 units, 40g key, 6g a week) or a **warehouse** (500 units, 120g key, 16g a week) in up to 5 towns. Its goods don't take slots in your packs.

- **Filling it.** Buy straight from the stalls into store (the price climbs as usual), or store goods from your packs.
- **Using it.** Take goods back into your packs, or sell them into *that* town's market at its price on the day. A sale never pays more than buying the same goods back would cost.
- **Speculation.** Fill it with grain in the harvest glut and sell in the lean weeks, or hold goods for a dearth.
- **Spoilage.** Goods keep twice as well as on a stall, but fish, meat and bread still spoil within weeks.
- **Rent.** Rent is due every 7 days. After two unpaid weeks, the landlord sells your stock off and lets the room to another.
- **Thieves.** They strike more often where the Thieves' Guild works, the more heat you carry in the realm, and with an outlaw lair or raiders nearby. A strong town watch and a guild rank of 2 or more protect you. A theft carries off 15–40% of the stock.

**Forward contracts.** A town will agree today to buy a good its people use, for delivery by a day 10–60 days away, at a price fixed now.

- **Price.** The price is what the lot fetches in the town today, plus a premium for the wait (3% + 0.2% a day, at most 15%). It is never more than buying the lot back here.
- **Size.** At most 15 days of the town's need.
- **Delivery.** Deliver in parts, from your packs or your store-room in the town. Each delivery is paid on the spot, and the goods join the town's stock.
- **Limits.** You can hold at most 3 contracts.
- **Missing the day.** A quarter of the undelivered value is forfeit, taken from your purse. You also lose reputation in the realm, and more of it if your purse can't cover the forfeit.
- **The bet.** If the town's price falls before the day, you deliver at the old, higher price. If it rises, you have sold too cheap.

### Market Fairs and Market News

Four fairs are held each year at the great towns:

| Fair | Day of the year | Where | Merchants bring |
|---|---|---|---|
| Spring Fair | 22, for 7 days | Capitals | Spices, luxuries, wine, clothes, tools, salt, horses |
| Midsummer Fair | 48, for 6 days | Capitals and cities | Wine, spices, cheese, cloth, beer, luxuries |
| Harvest Fair | 78, for 7 days | Capitals and cities | Grain, cheese, wool, hides, salt, horses |
| Frost Fair | 100, for 6 days | Capitals | Firewood, coal, clothes, liquor, lamp oil, salt |

Merchants from afar put their goods into the town's stores. The goods then sell, spoil and travel on like any other. While the fair is on, the stalls ask 6–8% less and pay **75–76%** of what goods are worth (not 70%). A sale still never beats buying the goods back. The heralds cry each fair, the tavern's merchants talk of fairs on or coming, and the Counsel points out one nearby.

Every week the heralds also cry the world's worst **food dearth** (a town in dearth with a staple at least 60% over its worth). In the weeks after the harvest they cry the deepest **grain glut** at a city or capital. A town is not cried again within 40 days.

### Labour, Classes and Migration

Every town's people are of five kinds, and they want different things. Open a town's **Trade** window and use its **👥 People** tab (the trade window's outlook and the holdings panel show a one-line summary). Tuned in `data/labour.json`.

| Class | Usual share (village → capital) | Works | Buys more of |
|---|---|---|---|
| 🌾 Peasants & labourers | 84% → 42% | The land, the mines, the woods, hauling | Grain, flour, beer, liquor, firewood, tools |
| 🔨 Artisans | 8% → 28% | The town's trades and the works you fund | Bread, beer, clothes, tools, coal, iron, cloth |
| ⚖️ Merchants | 3% → 15% | Its trade | Wine, spices, luxuries, cheese, meat, fine clothes, horses |
| ⛪ Clergy | 3% → 7% | — | Wine, lamp oil, candles |
| 👑 Nobility | 2% → 8% | — | Wine, luxuries, spices, gems, horses, weapons, fine clothes |

A town's mix leans from the usual one by its **peoples** (crafty peoples raise more artisans, enterprising ones more merchants, pious ones more clergy), by the **works** funded in it and a holding's **Workshop Quarter**. Migration and refugees move it further; it drifts back slowly.

**Demand.** What the town needs of each good is scaled by its mix against the usual one for its size. A town with the usual mix buys just what it always did. A court town full of nobles and merchants buys more wine and silk and less grain, and its prices follow.

**Work and wages.** Each class that works has a number of places (jobs) and a number of hands (half the peasants and artisans, 45% of the merchants).

- **Land.** The land takes every newcomer while it has room. It can employ a set number of people for the town's size, more on fertile ground; past that, each extra mouth finds only a third of a place.
- **Trades and trade.** Workshops and counting-houses are sized by the economy the town has built up, which follows its population slowly. Newcomers find no place at first, and a town emptied by war or plague is short of hands until it shrinks. Busy trades and busy trade roads want more hands; a trade cut by war leaves merchants idle.
- **Hiring.** Your works and your properties hire on top of that.
- **Short of hands.** A sector short of hands makes less: the land reaps and mines less, and the craftsmen, the town's trades and your works run only as far as the hands go.
- **Wages.** Wages float with the balance of places and hands (a third more where hands are scarce, less where they idle) and with the price of bread and fuel. Base wages run from 0.32g a day (a village labourer) to 1.04g (a capital merchant).

**Your hands.**

- **Properties** hire from the nearest town within 3 hexes (in the wild they bring their own). Half of a property's upkeep is its wage bill at that town's usual wage; it floats with the local wage. A town short of hands staffs it only as far as it can, and it produces that much less. Farms, mines and camps hire labourers; workshops, mills, bakeries, taverns and the like hire artisans.
- **Works** you fund hire artisans (a bakehouse 14 hands a level, a brewhouse 11, a mill 5). They are paid the town's craft wage as the works run, and a run must earn its wages as well as its margin. A skeleton crew (15%) is kept on while the works stand idle. The wages come out of your share; when the works earn less than their wages, the hands are paid from your purse.

**Idle hands** (more than about 6% out of work) sour the town's mood by up to 6 points, and breed **crime**, which also rises when the poor can't afford their bread.

- Crime makes the thieves' guild's jobs easier (up to +8% odds) and robs store-rooms more often.
- In a town you hold, crime and idle hands raise unrest every day.

**Welfare.** Each class's welfare (shown from *Thriving* to *Desperate*) weighs:

- its basket met: the food, drink, fuel, clothes, tools and luxuries the market stocks (a want the market doesn't stock counts as missing);
- its work;
- its wage against the price of bread;
- safety: sieges, a recent conquest, a realm at war with a neighbour's, outlaw lairs and raiders nearby, fire and flood;
- famine;
- your taxes, in a town you rule (heavy taxes weigh most on the peasants).

A town's welfare above or below the ordinary moves its mood by a point or two.

**Migration.** Every week each class drifts along the trade roads toward the linked town where it fares better: at most 1% of a town a week, the clergy and nobles slowest. A town you rule (a holding or colony) with land to spare draws settlers as if it fared better. Newcomers crowd the workshops and the land, so the flow slows as the gap closes.

**Refugees.** A town in danger or famine loses up to 5% of its people a week to linked towns that are safe, weighted by their size and welfare. The nobles flee first, the poor on foot. They remember home: once it is safe again, a quarter of them go back each week. A refugee column of 40 or more is cried by the heralds. A town crowded with refugees has idle hands and dear bread.

**Strikes and booms.** Artisans whose wages no longer buy their bread may strike for 7 days (towns and larger; not again within 45 days). Most of the town's trades and your works stand idle, and the heralds cry it. A thriving town that draws newcomers booms, and the heralds cry that too. The tavern talks of idle hands, refugees, strikes, booms and hard times. The Steward's Counsel warns when your works or properties can't find hands, and when a town you rule has many idle hands or is on strike.
### The Hinterland: Mines, Leases and Prospecting

The villages and countryside around every town specialise by their land. Open a town's **Trade** window and use its **⛏️ Hinterland** tab; the sites also show on the map as small icons beside their hexes (zoomed in), with a bar for what is left in a deposit and a gold ring round your own leases. Tuned in `data/hinterland.json`.

**Rural sites.** Each hex within 3 of a settlement belongs to the nearest one, and a town works its best few (2 for a village, up to 5 for a capital). An iron, coal, gem or gold deposit makes a mine, stone a quarry, salt a salt pan, timber a logging camp, a fish bank a fishery, horses a stud, cattle a ranch. About one hill or mountain hex in four hides a vein the locals know of even where the map shows none; **copper** is found only so (no map shows it), in hills, highlands and mountains. Open pasture and hills without a deposit run sheep.

| Site | Gives (a day, full hands) | Deposit |
|---|---|---|
| ⛏️ Iron mine | 1.5 iron | 700–2,000 iron |
| 🟠 Copper mine | 1 copper | 600–1,800 copper |
| 💎 Gem mine | 0.25 gems | 120–360 gems |
| 💰 Gold diggings | 0.25 gold ore | 120–360 |
| ⚫ Coal pit | 1.2 coal | 700–1,800 |
| 🧱 Quarry | 1.4 stone | 1,500–4,000 |
| 🧂 Salt pans | 0.9 salt | 1,200–3,000 |
| 🐑 Sheep run | 1 wool, 0.15 hides | — |
| 🪓 Timber camp | 1 wood, 0.3 firewood | — |
| 🎣 Fishery · 🐎 Horse stud · 🐄 Cattle ranch | 1 fish · 0.08 horses · 0.7 hides | — |

On the home town's market day each site sends what it dug half to its home market and half to the nearest market town within 6 hexes, so the villages supply the towns. Output follows the home town's land hands: a village short of peasants digs less. The weaving sheds, smithies and jewellers' rows of the towns nearby live on this wool, iron, coal and gems. Fewer cloth, tools and fine things now come from nowhere, so towns without the raw goods buy them from those that have them.

**Deposits run down.** Every unit dug comes out of the deposit. Past half the vein the yield falls, down to 35% at the bottom. An empty mine closes: its towns go short and the price climbs, and its miners leave for the nearest town. The heralds cry it and the tavern talks of it.

**Prospecting.** Every 60 days each town's hills are searched. About one time in ten a vein is struck: a waning mine gets a fresh one, or a new deposit opens on a hill or mountain, and miners come in from the next town. You can also **fund a search** yourself (160g, 8 days, about a 60% chance). Your strike is yours to lease at the reserve price for 20 days before the merchant houses may bid.

**Concessions.** The crown leases its deposits for 240 days.
- **Auctions.** Each realm puts one deposit up for auction every 30 days for 10 days. You can also **petition** for a lease on a deposit beside the town you stand in. Bids are held in bond. At the close the merchant houses bid what they think the lease is worth, the highest bid wins, and the fee goes into the realm's treasury. A losing bid comes back.
- **Running a lease.** You dig 1.3× the crown's rate, hire the hands at the home town's land wage (they are extra jobs there), and pay the crown a 12% royalty on every unit. Set the pace:
  - **Light** digs less and spares the vein.
  - **Steady** is the normal pace.
  - **Hard** digs 40% more but wastes the vein faster. Its pits can collapse (miners killed, the pit idle for 6 days, the town and the crown angry), and the hard pace sours the town.
- **Selling.** The ore is sold into the market town at 85% of what the lot is worth there on its scarcity curve. Or you can send it to **your own works** within 6 hexes (a smithy for iron, a bronze foundry for copper, a jewellers' row for gems, a weaving shed for wool), which use it before the town's stock.
- **Losing a lease.** You lose it at the end of its term, when the vein runs out, at war with the realm, under an embargo between it and the crown you rule or serve, after a default on its lenders, or when you cannot pay its wages and royalties for 14 days (that also counts as a missed payment on your credit). You also lose it when the town changes hands, or by royal edict if the crown's treasury is empty. A realm you rule can revoke a merchant house's lease.

The Steward's Counsel warns when your vein is running out or a lease owes money, and points to auctions nearby.

### Town Guilds

The people who work a town's trades band together in **guilds**. A town bakes, brews and smiths (the recipes above), so it has a **Bakers' Guild**, **Brewers' Guild**, **Smiths' Guild** and so on. The great towns also have a **Merchants' Guild** for spices, wine and salt. Visit them through the town's **⚒️ Guildhall** action or the trade window's **⚒️ Guilds** tab. The thieves' guild is a different kind of guild (see the Underworld). Tuned in `data/guilds.json`.

| Guild | Trades | Guards (its goods) | Buys at guild rates |
|---|---|---|---|
| Millers | mill | flour | grain |
| Bakers | bakery | bread | grain |
| Brewers | brewery, distillery | beer, liquor | grain |
| Colliers | charcoal kilns | firewood | wood |
| Weavers / Tailors | weaving / tailoring | textiles / clothes | wool / textiles |
| Tanners | tannery | leather | hides |
| Cheesewrights | dairy | cheese | meat |
| Smiths | smithy, forge, armoury, founder | tools, weapons, bronze ware | iron, coal, copper |
| Chandlers | chandlery | lamp oil | rock oil |
| Jewellers (cities) | jewellers | luxuries | gems |
| Merchants (cities of 5,000+) | — | spices, wine, salt | spices, wine, salt |

**How a guild lives.** A guild forms in a town (not a village) whose own trade is big enough for it. Its size follows the town's industry, which is weighed once a month. When the trade shrinks below half of what the guild needs for two months running, the guild dissolves. Each guild has a guildhall treasury fed by its members' dues, a named guildmaster, a standing with the crown, and its prosperity: whether its materials are on hand and its goods fetch a fair price.

**Its powers in its town.** These apply to the guild's own goods, in its own town:
- **The outsider toll.** If you are not a member, the guild takes **12%** of what you are paid (20% if it holds a royal charter). The toll goes into its guildhall. An apprentice pays half; a journeyman pays none. If you rule a realm whose court enforces *Merchant charters*, the guilds there may only toll half as much.
- **The sale cap.** An outsider may sell only about **5 days of the town's need** of the guild's goods a week. The guild's weekly turn resets the cap. Your trading company's factors sell as outsiders too: their sales count against the same cap (a post sells no more once it is reached) and pay the toll into the guildhall. A post closing down clears its stock past the cap, toll paid.
- **The price floor.** The stalls never sell the guild's goods below **80%** of their base ask (90% if it holds a charter), however glutted the market.
- **The guild's mark.** A master's goods carry the guild's mark and fetch **8%** more. No sale ever pays more than buying the goods back here.
- **Guild rates.** Journeymen and up buy the guild's materials **6%** cheaper.
- **Monopolies.** One monopoly can stand on a good in a town: the crown's (by the *Crown monopolies* edict), a merchant house's, your company's, or a guild's charter. Whoever holds it sets every guild's privileges on that good aside, except the holding guild's own. A charter already in place stands: a later crown edict fills only the empty slots. A guild's monopoly bars outsiders outright, but lets its journeymen and masters trade.

**The ladder.**

| Rank | Fee (× 1.5 city, × 2 capital) | Dues a week | To rise to it | What it brings |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Apprentice | 40g | 2g | Join in the guild's town (reputation −10 or better, no feud) | Half the toll; contracts; standing |
| Journeyman | 120g | 4g | 21 days as apprentice, standing 20, commerce 1 | No toll or sale cap; guild rates; your works there are protected |
| Master | 400g | 8g | 30 days as journeyman, standing 50, commerce 2, and a **masterpiece** | The guild's mark; loans; votes; guild-only works; credit backing |
| Guildmaster | — | — | Elected by the masters | A weekly stipend from the hall; call off a strike; more credit backing |

- **Standing.** It grows as you buy and sell the guild's goods and materials in its town, pay your dues, fill its contracts and run works of its craft there. It falls when you miss dues or fail a contract.
- **Dues.** They fall due on the guild's weekly turn. Three missed weeks and you are struck from its rolls.
- **The masterpiece.** Bring the guild's chosen goods in your packs, for example 30 grain and 6 salt for the bakers, or 12 iron and 8 coal for the smiths. They go into the town's market.
- **Contracts.** When the town is short of the guild's materials, the guild offers to buy 6 days of need (6–40 units) within 14–28 days. It pays 85% of what they are worth, and never as much as you would pay for them here. The hall sets the money aside when you sign. Fill the contract for +10 standing; fail it and you lose 15 standing and the guild grows sore with you.
- **Loans.** A master may borrow up to half the hall's treasury, at 6% scaled by your bank credit score (the same scale the banking houses use), repaid weekly over 8 weeks. Paid on time, it counts toward your credit history. Missed payments count against it; three missed payments are a default, which gets you struck from the rolls and marks your credit. Being a master adds **Guild backing** (+15, +30 as guildmaster, at most +45) to your credit report.
- **Elections.** Every 120 days the masters choose between the sitting guildmaster and a challenger. A master may back one of them (standing +5 if your side wins) or stand. Your chance rests on your standing and contracts, less any feud.
- **Guild-only works.** Only a master of the Chandlers' Guild may fund a **chandlery** (Merchant Prince → Invest).

**Guild politics.**
- **The squeeze.** Each week a guild weighs what squeezes it: war levies (+0.25), the crown's empty treasury (+0.4), heavy royal taxes (+0.35), a crown monopoly on its goods (+0.7) or a rival's (+0.5), its goods glutting the stalls (+0.45), dear materials (+0.35), the price of bread (+0.3, standing in for wages), an outsider's works undercutting it (+0.25) and outsiders flooding the stalls (+0.3). The squeeze is a running average.
- **Strikes.** After two weeks with the squeeze above 1, the guild may **strike**, for 10–18 days. Its trades in town stand idle and its craftsmen bring none of its goods to market, so the town really runs short: no bread in a bakers' strike, no tools in a smiths'. Prices climb, and whoever brings the goods in sells dear. A strike costs the hall each week and ends early if the hall runs dry. Each strike costs the guild crown standing. A crown that has lost patience may break a striking guild by edict.
- **Lobbying.** About once a month a guild with money lobbies the crown. A foreign crown weighs the guild's purse and standing: it grants a **royal charter** (higher toll and floor) or a **monopoly** on one of its goods. In a town you rule or hold, the guild brings you a **petition** with a purse: grant it the monopoly, or refuse and the purse goes home. A squeezed guild in your own realm also sours the realm's merchants.
- **Feuds.** If you are not a journeyman of the guild and your works in its town undercut it, especially while it strikes, it starts a feud with you. Flooding its stalls as an outsider, or your company holding a monopoly on its goods, does the same. A feud past 50 means its men wreck part of your works (8–20%). As a journeyman or better, your works are the guild's own. They are also harder for a crown to seize: half the chance (a third for a master), and twice the grace before expropriation.
- **The ruler.** If you rule the town (your realm, or a holding), you may **grant a charter** to a guild (founding one if the town has the trade but none) or **abolish** it. A charter brings a fee into the treasury and pleases the merchants. Abolishing a guild sends half its hall to the treasury and ends its privileges and monopoly; the town grumbles and the merchants sulk. An abolished guild does not come back unless chartered again.

The heralds cry strikes, elections, charters and abolitions. Townsfolk talk of a strike in their town. The Counsel points out guild contracts falling due, dues in arrears, a rank within reach and strikes nearby. There are achievements for becoming a journeyman, a master and a guildmaster.

### Kingdom Trade Policy

Every crown sets a **trade policy** for its realm: duties on what crosses its borders, bans on what may leave, embargoes on its enemies, treaties with its friends, and a state granary. You see it in the trade window (a line under the outlook, badges on each good, and the **⚖️ Tariffs** tab), and in the **Trade Policy** panel. Tuned in `data/tradePolicy.json`.

**Duties (tariffs).** A realm charges an **import duty** and an **export duty** on each kind of goods: grain & bread, fish/meat/salt, beer & liquor, fuel, raw materials, wares & tools, and luxuries. Duties are a share of the goods' value.
- **On the NPC trade.** The trade along the roads between two realms' towns must beat the duty as well as the haul. Less crosses, so the importing town stays shorter of the goods and pays more for them. The duty on what does cross goes into the treasuries: the import duty to the importer, the export duty to the exporter. Trade inside one realm pays nothing.
- **On caravans.** A caravan or trading ship crossing into a realm pays that realm's import duty on its cargo as its toll (never less than the customary 3%). A treaty waives it. An embargo doubles it.
- **On you.** The goods you buy are booked by the realm you bought them in. Sell them in another realm and you pay its import duty (the sell price shows it). Sell them at home and you pay nothing. Goods you came by otherwise (looted, crafted, from an old save) count as home goods.

**How a crown chooses.** NPC crowns weigh their policy on a weekly turn and hold a trade council once a month.
- **Stance.** A merchant or peaceful crown leans to **free trade** (about 2% on imports); a warlike or aggressive one to **mercantilism** (about 12%, more on wares and luxuries, with export duties on raw materials). Culture tips it (Maritime realms freer, Imperial ones more protective). It is reconsidered each year.
- **Treasury crisis.** An empty treasury, a debt over 60% of its means, or missed payments push every duty up (× 1.4, +3 points).
- **Guild lobbying.** Each guild in the realm's towns lobbies for its goods (+0.6 points of import duty per guild, at most +5).
- **Export bans.** When famine or a market dearth grips a quarter of a realm's towns, or its food costs 35% more than the world's (the weekly gazette's food index), its crown bans the export of grain & bread. Nothing of it leaves along the roads until three weeks have passed, no town is starving and food costs less than 15% over the world's.
- **Embargoes.** A crown embargoes every realm it is at war with, and lifts the embargo two months after the peace, once relations are better than −15. A bitter rival (relations −50 or worse) may be embargoed too. An embargo cuts the trade links between the two realms both ways and sours the other side (−8 relations). The realm's merchants dislike an embargo in peacetime (−1 stability).
- **Treaties.** Trading partners on good terms (relations 40, or allies at 15) sign **trade treaties**. Duties are waived both ways, their roads carry 20% more trade, relations warm a little each week (up to 50), and both realms are steadier (+1 stability). War or an embargo breaks a treaty; so do relations falling under 10.
- **Trade wars.** When a partner's duties on a realm's goods are higher than the realm's own on the partner's, by 4 points or more, the realm retaliates: +4 points on that partner's goods (up to +20). Each step costs both sides 3 relations, and every trade war costs the realm 1.5 stability. Trade wars cool when relations recover (above 30) or the other side backs down.
- **The state granary.** A crown with 1,500 gold or more buys grain where it is glutted (a quarter of the surplus, up to 60 in each of 4 towns a month), paying for it from the treasury. It stores up to 8 grain per 100 people (1% spoils each week). When its towns go hungry (a famine, the lean weeks with thin stores, a market dearth), it sends each of them a week's grain. Each fed town steadies the realm.

The heralds cry embargoes, treaties, trade wars, export bans and granaries opened, and townsfolk talk of them.

**As a merchant.**
- **Duties** are taken from what you are paid when you sell goods bought in another realm (see above). The crown banks the duty. Your books show it as *Import duties in your sale prices*. Licence fees show as *Tariffs*, smuggling takings as *Smuggling* and smuggling fines as *Fines*.
- **However you sell.** The booking travels with the goods, so the duty is the same whichever way they reach the buyer:
  - **Your caravans and trade routes** pay the destination realm's import duty out of their takings on arrival (twice it on goods from a realm the destination embargoed while the caravan was on the road). Smuggling routes dodge it, at their usual risk.
  - **Your ships.** Cargo bought at a port is booked to the port's realm; goods loaded from your packs keep their booking, and it comes back with them when you unload. Landing it at another realm's port pays that realm's import duty. A port under embargo with the cargo's realm buys none of it.
  - **Your trading company.** A factor's stock is booked to the realm it was bought in, and the booking goes with a convoy. A factor's sale pays the import duty on stock from another realm, and sells none of an embargoed realm's goods.
- **Export bans at the source.** A realm that bans the export of a kind of goods also stops it leaving by your hand: no caravan, trade route or company convoy carries it across the border, and no ship's hold is filled with it at its ports (the open sea counts as abroad). A licence from that crown lets it go. A route stalls until the ban is lifted.
- **Embargoed goods.** Goods you bought in a realm that this realm embargoes (or that embargoes it) can't be sold on the open market here. **Smuggle** them from the Trade Policy panel (1 AP). They sell for 25% more than an honest sale, but the watch may catch you. The chance starts at 12% and rises with your heat in the realm and the town's guards, and falls with your stealth. Caught, you lose the goods, pay a fine of half their worth, gain heat and may be jailed. Even a run that works raises your heat a little.
- **Export bans.** Selling abroad goods whose home crown bans their export (grain in a famine) is allowed, but word gets back: heat in that realm (0.4 per unit, at most 12 a sale).
- **Licences.** Petition a crown for a **trading licence** (150 gold plus 25 per town of the realm, 40% off if your reputation there is 25 or more; refused under −10 reputation or with heat of 40). For 120 days you pay none of its duties and may carry its banned goods out. The fee goes into its treasury.
- **Brokering treaties.** From the *All realms* tab, bring two realms that are not at war or under embargo, and are on speaking terms (relations 10 or better), to sign a treaty. You need reputation 10 with both. It costs 300 gold in gifts (split between the two treasuries) and 1 AP. You gain +15 House prestige and +5 reputation with each, and the two realms warm to each other (+10 relations).

**As a ruler.** Your Royal Court's realm tab opens your crown's policy. NPC turns no longer touch your duties or bans.
- **Duties.** Set the import and export duty on each kind of goods (0, 3, 6, 10, 15 or 25%). Cutting duties pleases the Guild Coalition (merchants faction) and raising them displeases it. The exception is raising the duty on goods your realm's guilds make (marked ⚒️): that wins their thanks. Your neighbours compare their duties with yours and answer high ones with their own trade wars. You hear of it, and so does the Steward.
- **Bans.** Ban or allow the export of any kind of goods.
- **Embargoes.** Lay or lift an embargo on any realm. Laying one costs 10 relations with that realm and annoys the Guild Coalition.
- **Treaties.** Propose a treaty. The other crown signs if its relations with you reach 40 (15 if allied); a good Chancellor lowers the bar by their competence. You may end a treaty, at a cost of 5 relations.
- **The granaries.** Tell them to fill, or stop buying. Grain already stored still feeds your hungry towns. This works alongside the famine dole.
- **Revenue.** The panel shows the customs taken last week and in all.

The Steward warns of embargoed goods in your packs, a licence about to run out, and, as a ruler, neighbours waging a trade war or laying an embargo on your realm.

### Price Knowledge: the Trade Map and the Merchant's Ledger

You do not see every market's prices from afar. You know a town's prices only from:

| Source | What you learn | How fresh |
|---|---|---|
| 👁️ Your visit | The whole market: asking price, what the stalls pay, stock | Each day you stand there |
| 🏪 Your trading company's factor | The whole market of each post's town | A report every 3 days |
| 🏰 Your steward | The whole market of each of your holdings | A report every 3 days |
| 🐫 A merchant met on the road | What its goods were worth at its origin and destination | As of the day it set out |
| 🍺 Tavern talk (bought, heard or from informants) | A few goods, as reported | As of the telling, and it may be wrong |

Every price you know keeps its age ("12 days old"). Talk only says what a good is worth, so the asking price is estimated from the town's size and the stalls' pay is 70% of it. Your book keeps up to 30 towns (the stalest are forgotten first). Tuned in `data/priceKnowledge.json`.

**The trade map.** Choose **📈 Trade Map** from the map modes (or press **M** until it comes up), then **G** to step through the goods (**Shift+G** back). Each town you know shows a marker with the good's last-known asking price, coloured by its stock (Glut blue to Dearth red), and the source and age of the news. Old news fades. Lines join the towns you know along the merchants' trade links: gold by land, blue by sea, thicker where caravans run often, and red and dashed where merchants shun a road after a loss. Caravans and trading ships in sight show the road ahead of them, bright if they carry the good.

**The Merchant's Ledger** (**📒 Ledger** in a town's trade window, or from the map-mode menu) has four pages:

- **📖 Price Book.** For each good, every known town's asking price, what its stalls pay, its stock, distance and the age and source of the news. **🗺️ Show on the trade map** opens the map on that good.
- **⚖️ Best Spreads.** The best known buy-here, sell-there margin for each good between two towns within 3, 5, 8 or 12 days' travel (about 3 hexes a day). It uses only what you know, so stale news can mislead, and a big lot moves the price against you.
- **📈 Price History.** The asking prices you saw in the 10 markets you visited last, a sample every 3 days.
- **💹 Profit & Loss.** Your books. First the **income statement** for the last 30 days (a season) or 120 days (a year): every gold flow you had, by source and grouped (trading margins; works, properties and company; interest and loans; dues, tolls and tariffs; taxes, office and faith; work and adventure; soldiers and ships; household). Below it the **capital and stock** you moved (goods bought and sold, works funded, properties built, money borrowed and principal repaid — not profit or loss), the guild tolls hidden inside your sale prices, and your **net worth**. Then your trading margins: each sale against the average cost of what you bought of that good (goods found, looted or made count as free), by good and by route (where you bought it → where you sold it). Every trading channel counts: the stalls, the ports, your store-rooms, forward contracts, guild contracts and the traders you hail on the road.

### The Economic Chronicle: the Gazette, Your Books and Fashions

**The ledger.** Every gold flow you have is kept by source, day by day, for 120 days (`data/chronicle.json` → ledger): sales and purchases at stalls and ports, store-rooms, forwards, caravans hailed on the road, escort pay, your works' share and their hands, store-room rent and key fees, property takings and upkeep, your trading company's profit and the dividends of the merchant houses it holds shares in, guild entry fees and dues (and the toll a guild takes from an outsider's sale price), loan interest (each payment is split into interest and principal), deposit interest and bond coupons, patronage, jobs and adventure. The Merchant's Ledger shows it as an income statement (**💹 Profit & Loss**).

**Net worth** = your gold + bank deposits + royal bonds + the goods in your packs, holds and store-rooms (at the best price your price book knows the stalls pay, or half their base price) + the book value of your properties (their cost, losing 10% a year down to half), your works (what you put in, as they stand), your ships and your trading company − loans, guild loans and rent owed. It is sampled weekly.

**The realms' gazette.** Once a week every realm's economy is measured over its towns, and the whole world's: a **food** index and a **cost of living** index (100 = goods at their base price; the stock in the stalls, the season and a debased coin all count — a crown that clips its coin shows as inflation at once), the goods its merchants move, how many towns are short of something or in dearth, the share of idle hands, how content the people are, and the crown's treasury, debts and coin. These numbers are public: a gazette is posted in every town with a market. Standing in one, you read it — your own realm's numbers as of this week, the other realms' a week older (news travels). What you know of single towns' prices stays what your price book holds.

**📰 The Economic Chronicle** (from the map-mode menu, the 📰 button in the Merchant's Ledger, or the Counsel):
- **🏛️ Realms.** Every realm you have news of side by side: food, cost of living and their change over the last four weeks, trade, towns short or in dearth, idle hands, treasury, debt and coin, and the gazette's date.
- **📈 Charts.** A realm's (or the world's) food, cost of living, trade, shortages, idle hands, welfare, treasury or debt over the weeks, and every realm's food side by side.
- **🏪 Shortages & Gluts.** The sharpest shortages and gluts in your price book, with the age and source of the news.
- **👗 Fashions.** The fashions you have heard of, where they began, the goods they make dear and how long they have left.
- **💰 Your Worth.** Your net worth by asset and debt, week by week.

**What the realms make of it.** A realm whose bread costs more than the world's grows restless (up to −6 on its stability target: *Cost of living*), and one whose bread is cheap is content (+2). A crown whose bread is dear is slower to march to war. The heralds cry it when a realm's bread grows dear and dearer than elsewhere, or its prices climb faster than the world's; townsfolk grumble that bread is up since spring, and the Steward warns you when bread is dear in a realm you know of.

**Fashions at court.** Now and then a court takes to a fashion — silks, spiced wine, furs from the north, jewelled finery, blood horses, perfumes and rare blooms, great feasts, carved ivory, or pious plainness. It starts at a capital and spreads outward a hex and a half a day, rising for a week, holding for three and fading over two, weaker the farther a town is from the court. Where it holds, the nobles and merchants (and a little the clergy and artisans) want more of its goods — or, for plainness, less — so those goods run short in the stalls and grow dear. Goods no market keeps — the exotic finds of the uncharted lands (pearls, sea silk, ambergris, ghost orchids, blue coral, golden amber, cloudpepper), silk and ivory from the ports — simply fetch more at the stalls and the ports while the fashion holds. The heralds cry a new fashion, the gazette lists the fashions within a few days' ride, and townsfolk and the tavern talk of it: whoever hears first can buy ahead of the demand. The Steward points out a fashion you have heard of, the more so when you hold its goods.

**Setting a fashion.** A patron with standing at a court can set its fashion (**Patronage & Great Works → 👗 Fashion at Court**, standing in a realm's capital): it takes 40 House Prestige and 400 gold in feasts and gifts — half with an artist or architect in your pay — and a standing of 20 with that realm (or its crown on your head). Your fashion is a quarter stronger and lasts half as long again, and the court tolerates one of yours every 60 days. Set the fashion for the goods you hold, then sell.

### Map Resources and Settlements

Resources on or within 2 hexes of a settlement shape what it produces and how it works (a resource on the settlement's own hex counts in full, a nearby one at half; each effect is capped at +50%). Open a settlement's **Economic Breakdown** to see its "Resource Advantages".

| Bonus | From | Effect |
|-------|------|--------|
| Goods | iron, timber, stone, coal, cattle | More of every good the settlement makes |
| Military | iron, horses, coal | Cheaper recruits for you (half the bonus); the realm's armies regrow faster |
| Trade | gems, spices, salt, horses, oil | More trade income from the goods merchants move through the town; caravans you send there earn half the bonus; sales there a quarter |
| Construction | timber, stone | Your properties, tech buildings and infrastructure cost less and build faster (half the bonus each); damaged towns repair faster |

Coal yields coal, oil yields rock oil, and cattle yield food and hides. Coal also fires forges, blacksmiths and glassworks on or beside it (+25% output), herds help cattle ranches, dairies and tanneries (+30%), and an oil seep helps a chandlery (+20%). Markets on coal, oil or cattle sell them cheaply.

**Processed Goods:**

| Good | Base Price | Made From |
|------|-----------|-----------|
| Flour | 10g | Grain (milling) |
| Bread | 15g | Flour (baking) |
| Smoked Fish | 20g | Fish (smoking) |
| Firewood | 12g | Wood (splitting) |
| Textiles | 25g | Wool (weaving) |
| Clothes | 40g | Textiles (tailoring) |
| Tools | 35g | Iron (forging) |
| Weapons | 60g | Iron (forging) |
| Beer | 12g | Grain (brewing) |
| Liquor | 30g | Grain (distilling) |
| Luxuries | 100g | Gold Ore (refining) |
| Leather | 18g | Hides (tanning: 1½ hides → 1 leather in a town's tanneries, 3 hides → 2 leather in your Tannery) |

### Banks, Credit & Coin

Money has its own life in the realms. A few great **banking houses** take deposits, lend to merchants and to crowns, and sometimes fail. Every realm strikes its own coin, and a crown in trouble may clip it.

**Banking houses.** Three to five named houses (the Medrano Counting House, House Varenne & Sons, …) keep their seats in capitals and great cities, at most one per realm, and the same world always has the same houses. A house serves you at its seat and at its **branches**, the cities and capitals of its realm. Look for the **🏦 bank** action there. Each house has capital reserves (your deposits are part of them, and its loans draw on them) and a standing with every crown. The Treasury's Bank tab lists the houses, their reserves and your accounts. The bank panel shows the full books: accounts, loans, royal bonds, your credit report and the realms' coin.

**Deposits** earn a slow, safe interest, compounded daily: about **4% a game year**, up to 7.5% at a house short of coin. They are not perfectly safe. If a house fails, its receivers pay back only **30–60%**. Deposits count as collateral, and the house you owe takes them first if you fall behind.

**Loans** come in four sizes:

| Loan | Amount | Base interest | Term | Credit needed |
|------|--------|---------------|------|---------------|
| Small | 500g | 10% | 30 days | any |
| Medium | 2,000g | 15% | 60 days | 520 |
| Large | 5,000g | 20% | 90 days | 600 |
| Great | 15,000g | 28% | 120 days | 680 |

The interest is a flat charge for the term, repaid in equal daily payments. Your **credit score** sets the actual rate: about a third less at 800 and a third more at 400. So do the house's reserves, since a house short of coin charges more. A bad name in the house's realm (reputation −20 or lower) adds 20%. The Merchant Guild tech cuts interest by 25%, and Banking Systems by another 20%. You may hold up to three loans from one house, within your **borrowing limit**: 60% of your collateral plus your gold, scaled by your score. You can repay a loan in full from anywhere.

**Your credit report** (300–850) is built from:
- **Repayment history:** loans repaid and days paid on time raise it. Missed payments, defaults (and recent ones in particular), and days overdue lower it. A debt made good wins some of it back.
- **Collateral:** properties at their build cost, ships, your trading company, holdings and deposits.
- **House Prestige.**
- **Debt load:** what you owe against what you own.

**Defaults have teeth.** A day you cannot pay adds a late fee and a day to the term. Then the arrears escalate:
- **7 days overdue:** the bailiffs take goods from your packs at half their worth, and your reputation in the house's realm falls.
- **14 days, then every week:** the house seizes collateral at half its worth, in this order: your deposits with it, your most valuable property, a ship lying in port (never the one you are aboard), then your company's coffers.
- **30 days:** you are **declared a defaulter**. The house writes off the rest and will not lend to you again until you **make good** the debt. You lose reputation in its realm (−15) and House Prestige. A debt of 1,500g or more puts a **bounty** on your head there. One of 4,000g or more sends you to the **debtors' prison** if the watch of any town in that realm catches you (5–20 days, with bail).

**Your own banking house.** In a city or capital where no house is seated, the **🏛️ Charter a Bank** action lets you found one (the bank panel's **🏛️ Your House** tab). The crown wants a charter fee (2,500g, 3,750g in a capital) and a reputation of 10 with the realm, and you pay at least 5,000g of capital into the vault (5,000, 10,000 or 25,000g). You may own one house at a time, and none while a house has you on its blacklist.

- **Deposits.** The townsfolk bring you their savings, drifting toward about 2.5g a head of the seat's people times their **trust**. Trust rises with your reputation in the realm and the house's age, and falls for 60 days after a run. You pay them the deposit rate (3–7.5% a year, more when your vault runs short).
- **Lending.** The house lends to merchants out of its vault. Your **policy** sets how much and at what rate:

| Policy | Lends | Rate | Ordinary losses | Lends to crowns |
|--------|-------|------|-----------------|-----------------|
| Cautious | 45% of its coin | 14% | 1.5% a year | no |
| Steady | 60% | 18% | 3% | yes |
| Bold | 85% | 24% | 7% | yes |

  Losses double while the realm is at war, and now and then a borrower goes under owing 4–10% of the loan book (twice as often when bold, half as often when cautious). Under Steady and Bold, crowns in need may borrow from your house like any other, and their repayments go straight into your vault.
- **Costs.** Clerks and the strongroom cost 2g a day plus a little for every 1,000g on the books.
- **The vault and the reserve.** The house must keep about 35% of its deposits (at least 1,500g) in the vault. Lending never touches the last half of that reserve.
- **The owner's desk** (at the seat only; the policy can be changed from anywhere): put more gold in, draw out anything above 120% of the reserve, or **wind the house up**. Winding up pays the depositors, calls in the loans at 85%, sells the crowns' debts to another house at 70%, and hands you the rest.
- **Runs.** Your house can suffer a run like any other, and is likelier to when the vault falls below half its reserve. During a run your depositors take about 12% of their deposits each day. When the vault is short, the house calls in loans at a loss; when there is nothing left to call, it **fails**. A failed house loses everything you had in it, costs you 20 reputation in its realm and up to 25 Prestige, and the realm gets a new house there about three months later. A house that pays out the whole run stands.
- Your house is not floated on the Bourse and keeps no insurance desk. Its equity (vault + loans + crown loans − deposits) counts in your **net worth**, and its books for the last 30 days are on the Your House tab.

**Royal bonds.** Through any banking house you can lend to a crown, from 500g up to 20,000g per crown, for 120 or 240 days. You get interest every 7 days, the principal back at maturity, reputation with the realm (+1 per 500g, up to +10) and Prestige for your House (+1 per 400g, up to +25). A healthy crown pays about **6% a year**. A crown deep in debt, with debased coin or a recent default, pays up to 20%, because it may miss coupons, put off repayment by a month, or **repudiate** its debts and cut your bond with everyone else's. You can sell a bond back to the brokers at a discount.

**Crowns in debt.** Every crown keeps a reserve of about a month's income. War costs more than a realm earns, and a crown at war (or raising a wonder) that drops below its reserve borrows from the friendliest house with coin to spare. It pays interest from its taxes and pays its debts down every week. A crown in crisis (missed payments, unpaid armies, or debts beyond what its income can carry) chooses one of three ways out:
- **A war levy:** more gold for two months, and unrest (stability −6).
- **Debasing the coin:** gold from the mint at once. The mint recoins the coin about at the lower standard, so there is more of it (see [Specie, Mints & the Price Level](#specie-mints--the-price-level)). Prices in all of the realm's markets rise (+9% for each tenth of silver taken out, and more as the extra coin spreads), stability falls, the houses think less of it, trust in its coin drops, and the good coin starts leaving for sounder realms.
- **Default:** its lenders lose capital and may face a run, and the realm loses stability (−10 at once, −8 while the memory lasts). No house lends to it for months.

Honest coin returns slowly once a crown is out of trouble, at once if new silver is found, or by a **recoinage** edict once the crown can pay for it.

**Panics.** A run on a house is rare, but more likely when its vaults are low, after a crown defaults on it, or after a crown seizes its strongroom. During a run, withdrawals are rationed to a trickle a day and no loans are made. When the run ends, the house either stands, poorer, or **fails**. A new house opens at the seat about three months later. Heralds proclaim failed houses, crown defaults and debased coin, and townsfolk talk about clipped coin, levies and queues at the counting-house doors.

**As a ruler**, the bank panel's Coin tab shows your crown's purse. From there you can borrow for the crown, debase the coin, or repudiate the crown's debts.

The Steward's Counsel warns you of an overdue loan, of the next step it brings and when, of gold running short of your daily payments, of a run on a house that holds your deposits, of bonds with a crown in trouble, and of a debtors' prison waiting for you.

### Underwriters, Bills & the Bourse

Merchants who move fortunes insure them, send them as paper, and trade shares in each other's houses.

**Underwriters.** Every banking house keeps an **insurance desk**, and an **underwriters' guild** sits at one or two of the great ports. Look for the **🛡️ Underwriters** action at a banking house, its branches, a guild's port, or any port of the guild's realm. You hold **open cover**, one policy for each kind of risk:

| Cover | What it covers | How the premium is reckoned |
|---|---|---|
| ⛵ Hull & cargo | Your ships away from port: cargo washed overboard or seized, hull damage (at its repair bill) | The season's storms at sea, hostile squadrons near the ship and the odds she loses to them, pirates in those waters, events on a voyage, and a worn hull (below 50% she leaks, and storms take more cargo) |
| 🐫 Goods on the road | Your caravans and your company's convoys | Raiders and outlaw lairs near each caravan (half on a road with wardens), a convoy's own risk |
| 📦 Stock in store | Your store-rooms and your company's posts | Each one's own weekly theft chance (guild towns, heat, lairs and raiders, guards) |
| 🏭 Works & property | Works damaged by a siege or a raid, or seized by a crown in crisis | Sieges, raiders, a crown in crisis |
| 🌾 Crops in the field | Your farms, pastures and orchards: the day's crop short of a fair-weather one, and the year's harvest short of a normal one (see [Harvests](#harvests-good-years-and-bad)) | The season's weather (or your field's own record, if worse), any drought or blight on the land now, and through the growing season the harvest forecast for the region |

Each day the cover bills a premium on what it actually covers that day: the expected loss, plus about a third. It pays each loss less a **10% deductible**. It attaches the day after you buy it (2 days for stock, 3 for works, 7 for crops), so you cannot insure a loss you already see coming. A poor harvest is claimed when it is reckoned, for the share of the growing season your cover was in force: cover bought in the last week of a failing summer pays only a week's share. A claim over 800g goes to the assessors for a few days first. Each claim you have made in the last 120 days adds 12% to every premium. War is not covered: a realm at war with you seizing your works is a war risk, and so is a town that turns on you.

Insurance is a fair bet that the house wins. Over a year it pays back about two-thirds of the premiums, so it is for sleeping at night, not for getting rich, and losing goods on purpose never pays (you get back 90% of a loss at best).

Underwriters can **fail**. They also insure other merchants, and those books bleed in wars, blockades, pirate seasons and the rare great storm. A guild whose capital runs out shuts its doors: its covers are void and its claims are paid at 30–60%. A new guild opens about three months later. A banking house pays its claims from its reserves, so a ruinous season can empty its vaults and start a run.

**False claims.** A member of the Thieves' Guild can buy forged protests of a loss on cover they hold: up to half of what is covered, at most 3,000g. The forger takes 20% up front. Every false claim is investigated. The assessors catch about 40% of them, less for a higher guild rank, more for each recent claim and for a claim near the cap. If you are caught, you get no pay and a fine as large as the claim, heat in the realm, your cover is cancelled, and the office will not insure you for 240 days.

**Bills of exchange.** Anyone robbed without an army loses 15–40% of the gold they carry. At a banking house (the 🏦 bank's ✉️ Bills tab), pay gold in and take a **bill** payable at another house's seat or branches. The fee is 0.6% plus 0.03% for each hex between the two seats (at most 4%, at least 5g). A bill is payable "at sight" after the courier's days (about 1 day for every 8 hexes), and should be presented within 120 days, after which a 5% protest fee applies.
- **Robbers take bills too.** Stop a stolen bill at a branch of the paying house (2%) before a forger cashes it (3–8 days), and it is yours again.
- **A bill on a failed house** falls on the house that drew it. If both have failed, the receivers pay 30–60%.
- **The guild's best hands** (rank 2 and above) can forge a bill on a house. Clerks see through about 40% of them, and one forgery a month is all the forger will do.

**The Bourse.** An exchange sits in each of the two greatest capitals (the **📈 Bourse** action there). Shares trade in:
- **the rival merchant houses** (100 shares each);
- **the banking houses** (1,000 each);
- **the chartered companies**: an overseas venture under a crown's charter, which lives by new lands (discoveries known to its crown, colonies) and dies by the sea (pirates, war, a fleet lost), and a crown's royal monopoly, whose rents follow its realm's trade and prices and which a crown in debt squeezes or, defaulting, may revoke;
- **your own trading company**, once you float it.

A share's worth is its **book value** times a multiple of its **return on capital**: a listing earning more than the market asks of its kind (12% a year for a merchant house, 8% for a bank, 14% for a venture) trades above its books, one earning less below it, and one losing money well below. The market pays only part of a high return up front, so a house that keeps earning it rewards the patient holder. For a banking house, book value is its equity (its reserves plus what the crowns owe it, less deposits), and low vaults, a run, or loans to crowns in crisis weigh on it. The price adds the crowd's mood, which drifts, sours in a **panic** (a house failing, a crown defaulting, war on the exchange's realm), and runs wild in a **mania**. A mania is rare: one listing is bid up two or three times its worth for weeks, the company sells new stock into it, and then the bubble bursts. The price falls well below its worth, the whole market's mood sours, and the bank that lent on the shares takes a loss. The heralds and the townsfolk talk of manias and crashes.

**Dividends** are paid every week from real profit: a house's from its trade, a bank's from the growth of its equity (out of its own reserves), a chartered company's from its profit. Buying and selling costs the spread (3%) and the broker (1%), and your own buying moves the price for a few days, so pumping a holding costs more than it makes. You may hold at most a quarter of any stock. A house you hold shares in that is bought out pays you its book value plus 15%. Shares in a house that is ruined, or a bank that fails, are worthless. **Spies** in a listing's realm tell you what it is really worth, which is an edge.

**Floating your company** (on the Bourse's ⚖️ Your Company tab) raises capital for it. The brokers buy shares at 10% under the company's worth and keep 3% of the proceeds. Outside shareholders then take 50% of each week's profit in proportion to their shares, and their part of anything you draw out: with 20 shares outside, drawing 800g costs the company 1,000g. You keep control up to 49 shares in 100, and you can buy them back on the floor.

The Steward's Counsel warns of a ship at sea in dangerous waters with nothing insured, a bill to present before it lapses or a stolen one to stop, and shares of yours riding a mania.

### Specie, Mints & the Price Level

Gold does not come from nowhere. Every realm has **coin in circulation**: the silver and gold in its people's purses, tills and strongboxes. The crown's chests and the banks' vaults hold more, but that coin moves no prices until it is spent. How much coin a realm has for its people sets its **price level**.

**The price level.** Each week a realm's coin per head is set against the world's. A realm awash in coin is dear, one drained of it cheap: its price level runs from 90 to 112 (100 is the world's norm), and it moves only part of the way each week. It multiplies every price in the realm's markets, buying and selling alike, together with its coin's purity: clipped coin raises prices on top. The weekly gazette's cost of living shows it as inflation or deflation (the Economic Chronicle shows each realm's coin level beside its debt and silver). Hoarded coin buys nothing: a realm at war hoards about an eighth of its coin, and one in a panic (a run on its bank, a crown default, or a panic on its Bourse) a tenth more, so its prices sag.

**Where the coin goes.** Each week:
- **Trade.** When the realms' merchants carry goods across a border, part of the balance is paid in coin. A realm that buys more than it sells loses coin, its prices fall, its goods grow cheaper abroad, and the coin flows back. A persistent deficit settles a few points under the norm; a surplus a few over.
- **Tribute and loans.** A realm that loses a war pays part of its tribute out of its people's purses. A banking house lending to a foreign crown sends its coin abroad, and the repayments bring it home.
- **Bad money drives out good.** When a crown clips its coin, merchants hoard the old full-weight pieces and send them abroad: the debased realm loses a little of its coin every week to its sounder trading partners.
- **Flight.** A run on a bank, or a crown that has just repudiated its debts, sends coin abroad to the soundest realm it trades with. So does a panic on a realm's Bourse: for 21 days after a bubble bursts, a house fails or the crown defaults there, and while the market's mood stands under 88% of its calm.
- **Your own trade.** Goods you buy in one realm and sell in another, from your packs or by caravan, ship hold or the company's factors, settle in coin as the merchants' trade does: the realm that bought them pays part of their value to the one they came from.
- **Wear.** Coin is worn, clipped, buried and carried off to far lands: a little is lost every week.

**The mints.** Every realm's mint sits at its capital. Each week it buys the gold and silver ore that stands in the realm's markets above what the towns want (from its own mines, bought in or brought by the merchants), and strikes it into coin. A debased mint strikes more coin from the same silver. Foreign coin that came in is brought to the mint and restruck. The crown keeps the **seigniorage** (5% usually), and the rest goes into circulation. A realm with silver and gold mines pours out coin and spends it abroad; one without lives on its trade. Silver mines are veins the locals know of in the hills and mountains (no map resource shows them).

**Selling bullion to the mint.** At a capital, the **💰 Mint** action shows the mint and every realm's coin. The mint buys gold ore, silver ore and gold bars at their fine worth (36g of coin in a unit of gold ore, 16g in silver ore, 140g in a gold bar: about their base price), less its seigniorage, at the realm's price level, and never for more than 95% of what the market there asks. It takes up to about 3,000g of fine metal from you a week. A mint where the coin is clipped or dear pays the most: bullion bought where coin is sound and cheap fetches its best price there, which is Gresham's law working for you, within the mint's weekly room and minus the changers' fee.

**Money changers.** Each realm's coin is assayed at the border. Crossing into a realm whose coin is of another purity costs a quarter of the purity gap on the gold you carry (at most 5%; a purse under 50g passes free): from a realm of 100% silver into one of 80%, 5%. Coins of the same purity change free. The Mint's 💰 Coin tab lists the rate of every realm's coin against the one you stand in, and the changers' fee. Your Ledger shows what the changers have taken.

**Leasing a mint.** At a capital you can lease its mint for 120 days: you pay up front 90% of half its recent seigniorage over the term (at least 40g), into the crown's treasury, and take half of its seigniorage each week. It is a small, steady income: most mints make 5–40g of seigniorage a week, and a mint in a mining realm, or one where foreign coin pours in, pays best. A war with the realm voids the lease.

**As a ruler**, the Mint's 👑 Your coin tab runs the realm's mint:
- **Seigniorage:** low (2%), usual (5%) or high (10%). A greedy mint keeps more of each piece, but sellers take their bullion elsewhere (it buys only 60% as much ore).
- **Recoinage:** call in the clipped coin and strike it anew at full weight. The crown makes up half of the missing silver from the treasury, and the holders lose the rest: there is less coin about and prices fall. Honest coin also restores trust and some stability. Not again within 120 days. Crowns out of trouble (little debt, no levy, and a treasury four times the cost) proclaim one by themselves.
- **Debasement:** clip the coin by a tenth (as in a crisis, see [Banks, Credit & Coin](#banks-credit--coin)).

The crown's taxes follow the coin a little: a realm drained of coin collects up to 3% less, one awash in it up to 3% more.

**False coin.** A Thieves' Guild hand of rank 1 or above can buy a batch of silvered copper from the guild's coiners (the Mint's 🕯️ Coiners tab, in any realm's town): stake 100–600g of good coin and clear 55% more if it passes. The watch catches about 16% of batches, more if your name is hot in the realm and less as you rise in the guild, and clipped coin is easier to fake. Caught, you lose the stake and pay a fine as large again, your heat rises and you may be jailed. One batch a week. Every false coin passed swells the realm's coin a little.

**News.** Heralds cry recoinages, coin shortages (prices well under the world's) and floods of coin. Townsfolk grumble about both, and merchants say where goods are cheap for want of coin. The Steward's Counsel tells you when a mint nearby would pay more for the bullion in your packs than the market, and, as a ruler, when your realm's coin is clipped, short or flooding.

### The Crown's Purse: Taxes, Wages & Tax Farms

Every crown keeps real books. Its gold is not conjured from its towns' prosperity: it is **taxed out of the towns' purses** (the coin their people hold: see *Town Markets → The coin is real*), and its wages and upkeep **go back into them**. The treasury is what is left over. A town with an empty purse pays nothing, whatever it owes.

**Revenue.** Each town's collectors come round once a week and take every day's dues since:
- **Land tax**: a share of the peasants' earnings (more in a bumper year, less in a failed one) and of the town's production gold (an assessment of what it makes, not coin), half of the coin the town took for the goods it sold to other towns, and 1% a day of the coin it holds past 2 gold a head.
- **Hearth tax**: so much for every working head. Clergy and nobles are exempt; merchants pay half as much again.
- **Excise**: a share of the artisans' earnings and the merchants' trade income.
- **The demesne**: the crown's own estates, a share of the land's gold (the capital's counts double), and the crown's monopolies.
- **Customs and tolls** (trade policy, public works), **seigniorage** (the mint), **tax farms sold**, an **extraordinary levy** while one runs, and **offices and crown land sold**.
- How much reaches the chests depends on the steward, the realm's stability, blockades, events and the coin about, less what **corrupt officials** keep.

**Spending.**
- **Army wages**: every soldier is paid daily, at 1.6× in the field, plus a bounty for each recruit. The soldiers spend their pay where they are quartered: by people, weighted 3× in the capital, 2× in a city, 1.5× in a town and 0.4× in a village.
- **Court and administration**: a base, more per town and per head. The clerks spend it in the capital.
- **Roads and works**, **debt service**, the **famine dole and granaries**, and the **ruler's purse** (the royal levy).
- Whatever else moves the treasury (events, tribute, temples, the fleet) shows as **Other**, so the books always balance. The crown's builders, shipwrights, wardens, cartographers and grain dealers are paid in the capital: the coin goes into its purse.

**Arrears.** What the chests can't pay is owed. Soldiers unpaid for more than a week desert: the army the realm can keep shrinks by about 1% a day, to 60% at worst. Each unpaid day also costs stability, and after three weeks the heralds cry a mutiny. Unpaid clerks raise **corruption**, which leaks taxes and weighs on the people. Back pay is paid first when money returns, and deserters return within days.

**Tax levels.** There are four: Light (×0.75), Standard, Heavy (×1.3) and Crushing (×1.65).
- Heavier taxes fill the chests but raise the **burden** the people feel. That lowers each class's welfare (so the poor drift to lighter realms), lowers the realm's stability ("Taxes & pay"), and breeds tax revolts.
- NPC crowns review their level about monthly:
  - Their temper sets the starting point: peaceful and mercantile realms tax lightly, aggressive ones heavily.
  - They tax more at war, when the chests are low or the soldiers unpaid.
  - They tax less when rich, when the realm is restless, or after a tax revolt.
  - A rich court turns lavish, a broke one frugal and on half pay.

**Tax revolts.** Each week, a heavily taxed town whose peasants are hard-pressed may drive out the collectors. Its taxes are withheld for 30 days, it loses happiness, and the realm loses stability. A tax farmer's squeeze makes revolts likelier.

**Tax farming.** A crown short of money (low chests, weeks in deficit, at war, or its soldiers unpaid) may auction the taxes of one of its towns (never the capital) for 120 days.
- The lot is reckoned at the town's recent taxes over the term. A banking house bids 82–92% of that, and the lot closes after a week.
- The farmer collects the town's taxes in the crown's place, and squeezes: a house takes 1.25×.
- **You can bid** from any town of that realm (🧾 Treasury → Tax farms). Your bid is held until the lot closes and returned if you are outbid or the auction is called off.
- Choose how hard your collectors press. Your men take 5% for their trouble.

| Squeeze | Take | Reputation in the realm | The town's happiness |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gentle | ×1.05 (you barely clear the stake) | none | none |
| Firm | ×1.2 | −0.02 a day | −0.01 a day |
| Harsh | ×1.4 (rich, and hated) | −0.06 a day | −0.05 a day |

A conquered town's new masters do not honour your farm, and a tax revolt drives your men out for a month.

**Fiscal crises.** A crown at war, in deficit or with its soldiers unpaid borrows from the banking houses (the Bank). It is in crisis when:
- it misses debt payments,
- its soldiers go two weeks unpaid,
- its debt runs past its capacity (25 days of revenue), or
- it spends eight weeks in deficit with the chests below a month's reserve.

In crisis, it raises an extraordinary levy (an extra 30% tax for 60 days), debases its coin (the crown keeps the extra pieces struck), sells offices (20 days' revenue now, corruption for years), sells crown land (the demesne shrinks for good), or defaults.

**As a ruler**, the 🧾 Treasury (from the Royal Court's Realm tab, or any town of your realm) shows:
- the budget line by line, yesterday and last week;
- the treasury's year as a sparkline;
- the people's burden, corruption, arrears and the demesne.
- the crown's books all told: taxes and dues taken, spending, tax revolts, tax farms, offices and crown estates sold, and soldiers who deserted for want of pay.

There you set:
- each tax apart (land, hearth, excise);
- the army's pay: half pay is cheap but the army shrinks to 88%; generous pay costs 25% more for a loyal 105% army;
- the court: frugal breeds corruption, lavish adds stability.

You can also put a town's taxes to auction, and sell offices or crown land. Your royal levy is a share of the crown's daily taxes. The 🏰 Realms tab compares every realm's purse.
### Land, Estates & the Putting-Out System

**The farmland and its estates.** The grain, flax and vines a town brings to market grow on the farmland about it. About 40% of that land is the peasants' own freeholds and commons, which nobody sells. The rest is held in a few **estates**: one about a village, two about a town, three about a city, four about a capital (the first of a capital's is the crown's demesne). They are held by the crown, the realm's noble houses, the Church, the town's burghers or the merchant houses. The **🌾 Estates & Land** action in every settlement (also the 🏡 Estates tab of the market window and the Steward's Counsel) shows each estate: its holder, its share of the farmland, its tenant households, its soil, its crops a day and their worth, its rent, its tenants' loyalty and what it is worth.

An estate is a share of the town's own fields, not extra land: its crops are already in the town's market. What a holder does to it changes the market. Drained or marled fields grow more, and the town reaps more. Enclosed fields grow wool instead of grain. When the cottagers spin out of season, fewer hands are in the fields. An estate left as it was changes nothing.

**Rent.** The tenants pay once a month, on the town's own day:
- **Level.** Low (18% of the crop), customary (25%) or high (34%). The tenants' loyalty heads toward 78, 55 or 26.
- **In coin or in kind.** A coin rent is a fixed sum on the estate's usual crop. In a bad year it can be more than the tenants can hand over (half the month's crop at most), and the rest falls into arrears: loyalty falls. In a good month the tenants pay some of it back out of their spare crop (half of what they could hand over above the rent, at their loyalty's collection rate), and the bailiff writes off a tenth of what is left; the Ledger books it as *Estate arrears collected*. The estate card shows each month's rent *due* and *paid* (and how much of it was arrears). **Press for arrears** sends the bailiff once a month: he takes up to 60% of what is owed (no more than a month's crop capacity, nor than the town's coin), and the tenants lose 12 loyalty. If that leaves them under 35, they may riot instead (the chance is shown on the button, up to 90%): a **rent riot**, nothing collected, the town's mood falls and a quarter of the arrears is written off. A **share of the crop** follows the harvest and its price, so the landlord carries the bad year; the tenants like it better (+6 loyalty).
- **Loyalty.** Tenants at loyalty 0 pay 70% of what is due; from 75 they pay all of it. Under 20 they may withhold a month's rent (a rent strike) or riot. Under 25 they sour the town a little each month; over 70 they cheer it.
- **High rents drive people away.** Each month about 3% of an estate's households leave for the nearest town, more as loyalty sinks. Pay piece wages in the town (below) and the tenants are more content.
- **Where the gold comes from.** Your rents come out of the town's own coin, the wealth its crown taxes, so a landlord's rent is the town's money, not new money. A steward and bailiff keep 8%. The Ledger shows rents as *Estate rents*.

**What land is worth.** An estate is priced at its expected yearly net rent over the realm's **land rate**. The land rate is 5.5% plus half of how far the crown's bond rate stands above 6% (Banks, Credit & Coin). It rises a point while the realm is at war and another while it is in unrest, and stays between 4% and 9%. Free towns' land goes at 6.5%. So land pays its buyer 4–8% a year, about the rate of a deposit or a royal bond, in rent instead of interest. It is safe from robbers and keeps its worth as prices rise. A village estate is worth about 1,500–7,000g (small hamlets less), a town's 7,500–24,000g, and a capital's 25,000–50,000g.

**Buying and selling.** Land comes on the market when its holder needs money:
- Noble houses live a little beyond their rents, and some years they spend far more. Once a house's debt passes 8% of its land's worth, its cheapest estate goes on the market at 92% of its worth.
- A crown deep in debt, or with an empty treasury at war, sells a crown estate now and then (at 95%).
- A listing waits a week for you. Then the richest purse able to pay takes it: a merchant house, the town's burghers, a noble of the realm, now and then the Church. If nobody buys, the price falls 8% a month.
- You may also make an **offer** on an estate that is not for sale. Nobles ask 1.15× its worth, houses 1.1×, burghers 1.2×. The crown asks 1.25× and sells only to someone it trusts (reputation 10). Indebted holders ask 5% less. **The Church never sells.**
- The crown takes a **3% duty** on every purchase.
- To sell, take the quick price (90% of the worth) from the richest buyer about, or list the estate at 95% and wait. Buying and selling back at once always loses.

**Improvements.** Paid up front; most of the cost is the hands' wages, which go to the town:

| Works | Cost | Days | Effect |
|---|---|---|---|
| Drain the wet fields | 12g a household (at least 60g) | 30 | Soil +10%, once; tenants +3 loyalty |
| Marl the fields | 5g a household (at least 30g) | 15 | Soil +6%, wearing off 2 points a year |
| Enclose for sheep | 6g a household (at least 40g) | 20 | The fields run sheep: wool worth 55% of the crops at base prices, let at 60% to a grazier |
| Re-let to tenants | 3g a household (at least 30g) | 20 | Undoes an enclosure |

Soil is capped at 130%. **Enclosure** pays where wool is dear and grain cheap. Three households in four are turned out to the nearest town of the same size or larger, where they swell the labourers. The town reaps less grain and more wool. The tenants who stay lose 15 loyalty, and the cottagers may riot: the chance is (60 − loyalty) / 60, between 10% and 90%. Noble houses and merchant houses enclose too, at sowing, where wool is dear.

**War, conquest and marriage.**
- **Peace terms.** A realm that loses a war cedes its best crown estate to the victor half the time.
- **Conquest.** When a town changes hands, the old crown's and nobles' estates there pass to the new crown. Yours are **confiscated** if your reputation with the new realm is under −20 or you are at war with it.
- **Bequests and dowries.** Nobles leave estates to the Church, and estates pass between noble houses as dowries.
- **Your own dowry.** You may settle one of your estates on a realm's nobility as a dowry: +1 reputation there for each 150g of its worth (at most +25), and House Prestige (+1 for each 500g, at most 10).
- **Landed gentry.** Holding land brings the House a little Prestige each month (1 an estate, at most 3).

**The putting-out system.** The **🧶 Put Out Work** action (or the market window's 🧶 Putting-out tab) puts out raw **wool** or **flax** that you carry.
